<!-- b000005 | furniture | pages [1] -->
2504218

<!-- b000006 | title | pages [1] -->
A

<!-- b000007 | heading | pages [1] -->
Summary Sheet

<!-- b000008 | title | pages [1] -->
Where Footsteps Collide: A Spatiotemporal

<!-- b000009 | title | pages [1] -->
Journey through Staircase Wear

<!-- b000010 | title | pages [1] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000011 | heading | pages [1] -->
Summary

<!-- b000012 | prose | pages [1] -->
Stones and other materials in historic building steps undergo continuous, long- term wear, a process archaeologists examine to uncover valuable insights. To sup- port archaeological research and long-term maintenance, we propose a wear model for steps and investigate how measurement data can invert key parameters within that process.

<!-- b000013 | prose | pages [1] -->
Several models are established: Model I: Hybrid Wear Model; Model II: Wear- parameter Inversion Model, etc.

<!-- b000014 | title | pages [1] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000015 | heading | pages [1] -->
Before building the models, we identified archaeologists’ data collection method:

<!-- b000016 | prose | pages [1] -->
using 3D scanners to create point cloud maps, which is cost-effective and non-destructive. We also gathered data through calculations and set reasonable parameters.

<!-- b000017 | prose | pages [1] -->
For Model I, we introduced three submodels that form a hybrid wear framework. Sub-model i, grounded in Archard’s theory and PDEs, provides a probabilistic equa- tion for archaeological contexts, laying the foundation to describe stair-wear dynam- ics. Sub-model ii then incorporates a single-parallel hybrid approach, showing how different single/parallel usage ratios influence final wear through foot traffic; by ex- amining how those ratios relate to foot traffic, we determine the influence of stair usage frequency on final wear. Finally, sub-model iii, based on human dynamics, extends sub-model ii to produce a 3D wear-depth distribution, applying Gaussian blurring and random offsets to generate realistic footprints.

<!-- b000018 | prose | pages [1] -->
For Model II, we devised a comprehensive inversion approach that uses mea- surement data to estimate optimal wear parameters, taking the hybrid wear equation from Model I as the forward model. We incorporate stair-material and archaeologi- cal insights in a Bayesian framework by assigning prior distributions to multiple pa- rameters. The likelihood function is formed from the residual between measurement and simulation. After Particle Swarm Optimization (PSO) locates the global optimum, Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) refines the posterior distribution in its vicinity, capturing parameter uncertainty.

<!-- b000019 | prose | pages [1] -->
• Tasks 1, 2, and 3 show a ±5% difference in ascending/descending, with peak foot traffic at 30%-40% and usage frequency uncertainty of ±5%-10%. • Tasks 1, 2, and 4 align model wear predictions with archaeological data, with depth error of ±0.015m and material wear uncertainty of ±10%.

<!-- b000020 | prose | pages [1] -->
For the advanced tasks 3 & 5, we developed a multi-layer PDE model as well as a discrete-event simulation model to handle repeated repairs and typical one-day stair wear. Finally, we performed a sensitivity analysis. The results show that our model remains stable.

<!-- b000021 | keywords | pages [1] -->
Keywords: PDE, Archard’s theory, PSO, MCMC, Bayesian framework

<!-- b000711 | prose | pages [3] -->
1 Introduciton

<!-- b000712 | prose | pages [3] -->
1.1 Problem Background

<!-- b000713 | prose | pages [3] -->
Ancient buildings hold immense significance for archaeological research, particularly their steps, which have borne witness to the erosion and passage of time. The condition of these weathered stairs offers valuable insights[1]; on one hand, they allow us to deduce the frequency and usage patterns of people from the past. On the other hand, as integral components of the structure, the wear and tear of the steps can inform us about the duration of their use, the state of repairs undertaken, and essential details regarding the construction materials employed. For archaeologists, conducting field investiga- tions and measuring architectural steps can serve as a crucial guide in the exploration of architectural history.

<!-- b000714 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b000715 | prose | pages [3] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b000716 | caption | pages [3] -->
Figure 1: Worn stairs in famous ancient buildings

<!-- b000717 | prose | pages [3] -->
As illustrated in the figure above, the image depicts the concave state of the steps that have been formed as a result of repeated foot traffic. The wear of steps is influenced by a variety of factors.[2] First, according to the principles of engineering mechanics, different materials exhibit varying degrees of deformation when subjected to force. This variability in deformation is further compounded by the dispersive effects of the forces applied. Secondly, factors such as walking speed, frequency of foot traffic, and individual walking habits contribute to the differential wear experienced by the steps.

<!-- b000718 | prose | pages [3] -->
1.2 Restatement of the Problem

<!-- b000719 | prose | pages [3] -->
In light of the aforementioned context, we aim to gather pertinent information regarding the wear patterns of building steps through field measurements. Our objective is to assist archaeologists in de- riving historical insights by investigating the correlation between step wear and user behavior. To this end, our research will focus on addressing the following questions:

<!-- b000720 | prose | pages [3] -->
• Task 1: Establish a model to simulate the distribution of step wear under the condition of stepping for a certain period of time.

<!-- b000721 | prose | pages [3] -->
Establish an appropriate function, which can simulate the deformation of steps made of different materials after a certain amount of wear by changing the correlation coefficient, and obtain the corresponding image.

<!-- b000722 | prose | pages [3] -->
• Task 2: Explore the state of use of the steps.

<!-- b000723 | prose | pages [3] -->
Based on the established function of the depressed portion, the correlation coefficients are ob- tained given the existing situation of the steps, and then the walking behavior of people on the steps is inferred:

<!-- b000724 | prose | pages [3] -->
- Investigate the frequency of foot traffic on the steps.

<!-- b001413 | furniture | pages [4] -->
Page 3 of 25

<!-- b001414 | prose | pages [4] -->
- Explore the direction in which people are accustomed to walking on steps.

<!-- b001415 | prose | pages [4] -->
- Investigate the usage density of the steps, that is, the characteristics of people walking side

<!-- b001416 | prose | pages [4] -->
by side or in single file under the same conditions.

<!-- b001417 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Task 3: Determine the necessary data to be obtained through observation and measurement.

<!-- b001418 | prose | pages [4] -->
Explore the conditions under which the wear of the steps can be investigated on-site, and aim to acquire the data in the simplest possible way, identify which data needs to be collected.

<!-- b001419 | prose | pages [4] -->
• Task 4: Analyze the consistency with the actual situation.

<!-- b001420 | prose | pages [4] -->
Under the conditions that the estimated number of years the steps have been in existence and the estimated use of the steps by people are known, etc., the depth and type of wear and tear are extrapolated to analyze the consistency with the actual situation, including the following aspects:

<!-- b001421 | prose | pages [4] -->
- The reliability of the estimated number of years of use of the steps.

<!-- b001422 | prose | pages [4] -->
- The consistency of the repairs made to the steps with the records of refurbishment.

<!-- b001423 | prose | pages [4] -->
- The consistency of the materials used for the steps with the wear and tear characteristics of

<!-- b001424 | prose | pages [4] -->
the types of sources from which they are actually made.

<!-- b001425 | prose | pages [4] -->
- Consistency between the frequency of daily use of the steps obtained from the exploration

<!-- b001426 | prose | pages [4] -->
and the crowd flow patterns.

<!-- b001427 | prose | pages [4] -->
1.3 Our Work

<!-- b001428 | caption | pages [4] -->
Figure 2: Overview of this work

<!-- b001429 | prose | pages [4] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b001430 | furniture | pages [4] -->
2

<!-- b001431 | furniture | pages [4] -->
MATHmodels

<!-- b001432 | furniture | pages [4] -->
获取更多资讯

<!-- b001435 | prose | pages [5] -->
2

<!-- b001436 | heading | pages [5] -->
Model Preparation

<!-- b001437 | prose | pages [5] -->
2.1

<!-- b001438 | heading | pages [5] -->
Assumptions and Justifications

<!-- b001439 | heading | pages [5] -->
1. Assumption: Ignore significant behavioral differences due to weight bearing or jumping, etc.

<!-- b001440 | prose | pages [5] -->
Justification: Assuming that the walker’s weight is normal and the pace is evenly distributed. Ignoring the effects caused by particular behaviors, in reality, the process of going up the stairs leads to wear and tear phenomena due to individuals with random differences that are contin- gent and meaningless to discuss.

<!-- b001441 | heading | pages [5] -->
2. Assumption: The steps are assumed to be ideal horizontal planes, and any surface inclination

<!-- b001442 | prose | pages [5] -->
caused by depressions in the stairs can be neglected. Justification: In the context of archaeology, long-term foot traffic, varying walking patterns, and environmental erosion contribute to increased surface complexity, unnecessarily complicating the analysis. Therefore, the idealized horizontal plane assumption simplifies the problem.

<!-- b001443 | prose | pages [5] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b001444 | heading | pages [5] -->
3. Assumption: Pedestrians are more inclined to walk in the middle of the steps, while the fre-

<!-- b001445 | prose | pages [5] -->
quency of walking on the sides is relatively low. Justification: By assuming that pedestrians prefer to walk in the middle position, the study fo- cuses on the most common walking patterns, avoiding unnecessary complexity in the model due to the rare use of edge positions, making the model more practical.

<!-- b001446 | heading | pages [5] -->
4. Assumption: The rolling friction between the soles of pedestrians’ shoes and the stairs involves

<!-- b001447 | prose | pages [5] -->
a fixed minor slipping, which can thereby be equated to sliding friction. Justification: In scenarios like stair stepping or wheel rolling, surfaces often experience “nominal rolling contact,” but in reality: a) microscopic sliding occurs due to material roughness, causing wear; b) local unloading-reloading happens with each step. After analysis, simplifying foot con- tact as micro-sliding wear may be acceptable in archaeology.

<!-- b001448 | prose | pages [5] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b001449 | prose | pages [5] -->
2.2

<!-- b001450 | heading | pages [5] -->
Notations

<!-- b001451 | caption | pages [5] -->
Table 1: Important notations.

<!-- b001452 | heading | pages [5] -->
Notation

<!-- b001453 | prose | pages [5] -->
Description

<!-- b001454 | prose | pages [5] -->
h wear height p average pressure S area of application κ combined coefficient β environment constant N Poisson process arrival rate α(N) probability of parallel usage of stairs δ mean lateral distance between left and right footprints σ width of the single-foot peak Θ parameter vector p(Θ) Bayesian prior p(Θ|·) Bayesian posterior

<!-- b001455 | prose | pages [5] -->
2.3

<!-- b001456 | heading | pages [5] -->
Data Collection and Preparation

<!-- b001457 | prose | pages [5] -->
In order to accurately represent the three-dimensional wear profile of stair treads in our mathe- matical modeling, we focus on measuring the following quantitative indicators and expressing them in concise mathematical form. Suppose the stair plane coordinates are given by (x, y) ∈Ω⊂R2, and let z denote the height (or depth of wear) in the vertical direction relative to some reference plane.

<!-- b002147 | heading | pages [7] -->
Model I: Hybrid Wear PDE Model

<!-- b002148 | prose | pages [7] -->
3

<!-- b002149 | prose | pages [7] -->
Submodel i: Probabilistic Wear Equation (PWE) 3.1

<!-- b002150 | prose | pages [7] -->
This paper focuses on the long-term step wear, particularly edge depression caused by pedestrian footfall. While real wear mechanisms involve nonlinear factors (e.g., adhesion, fatigue, temperature), their average effects are effectively captured by Archard wear theory[3] on a macroscopic scale over extended periods.

<!-- b002151 | prose | pages [7] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b002152 | prose | pages [7] -->
Below, the PWE is derived directly from the Archard wear formula, undergoing reasonable equiv- alent and equivalent transformations, and possesses a solid theoretical foundation and practical appli- cability.

<!-- b002153 | prose | pages [7] -->
To prepare, the Archard wear formula is demonstrated:

<!-- b002154 | prose | pages [7] -->
V = KFs, (1)

<!-- b002155 | prose | pages [7] -->
where V represents the wear volume, wear coefficient K is related to material hardness and roughness, F is the normal load, and s is the relative sliding distance of the material.

<!-- b002156 | prose | pages [7] -->
In the context of archaeology, local peak loads on the sole, load variation over time (supporting leg vs. free leg), and additional inertial loads during dynamic movement could be reasonably neglected.

<!-- b002157 | prose | pages [7] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b002158 | prose | pages [7] -->
Then, assuming a constant normal load F and uniform height decrease due to micro-volume wear, let the pressure per unit area S be p = F/S. Taking the difference of both sides of (1) and dividing by S, we have:

<!-- b002159 | prose | pages [7] -->
∆h = ∆V

<!-- b002160 | prose | pages [7] -->
S = Kp∆s.

<!-- b002161 | prose | pages [7] -->
(2)

<!-- b002162 | caption | pages [7] -->
Figure 4: xxx.

<!-- b002163 | prose | pages [7] -->
Next, we analyze the wear of steps. Firstly, take account of the one-dimensional case, treating the step as a one-dimensional coordinate system. Consider the cumulative wear height dh near point x over time dt. From the assumptions, a single footstep can be equivalent to a fixed small sliding ∆s, with a uniformly applied load F over an area of contact S. Denote the average pressure by p = F/S. In a time interval dt, there are Ndt footsteps, and the total wear height is given by

<!-- b002164 | prose | pages [7] -->

<!-- b002165 | prose | pages [7] -->
∆h = Kp∆sNdt.

<!-- b002166 | prose | pages [7] -->

<!-- b002167 | prose | pages [7] -->

<!-- b002168 | prose | pages [7] -->

<!-- b002169 | prose | pages [7] -->
Assume that the wear position is distributed along the transverse direction with a probability distribu- tion Φ. When pedestrian flow becomes very large and results in crowding, people’s available walking space is inevitably restricted, leading to changes in the wear distribution. Consequently, we further assume that the arrival intensity N also affects the wear distribution, and thus write Φ = Φ(x; , N). It follows that local wear height is given by

<!-- b002170 | prose | pages [7] -->
dh(x) = Φ(x; , N)dhtot.

<!-- b002171 | prose | pages [7] -->
(4)

<!-- b002174 | prose | pages [8] -->
Then, align equations (2)-(4), immediately obtaining PWE, the core result of this subsection:

<!-- b002175 | prose | pages [8] -->

<!-- b002176 | prose | pages [8] -->
= Kp∆sNΦ(x; , N) = κNΦ(x; , N), dt (5)

<!-- b002177 | prose | pages [8] -->
where κ = Kp∆s is a combined coefficient, which is inversely proportional to the hardness of material and directly proportional to the average pressure.. In equation (5), the coefficient κ is influenced by material hardness and contact area (or equivalently, pressure). Below, we will demonstrate that the probability distribution Φ(x; , N) primarily determines the spatial variation of wear, highlighting how different landing patterns concentrate or distribute wear along the transverse direction.

<!-- b002178 | prose | pages [8] -->
3.1.1 Generalized PWE

<!-- b002179 | prose | pages [8] -->
According to the literature, the wear sustained by a step is mainly due to the crowd’s trampling (depending on factors such as pedestrian flow rate and footstep distribution) and environmental fac- tors (such as rainfall, weathering, or particle impact)[4]. Here, we simplify the step to a lateral width interval [0, W], with the wear depth h distributed along the coordinate x. No variation or diffusion coupling occurs in the longitudinal direction y. Thus, at any given moment, the relationship between the horizontal position on the step surface and the amount of wear in the depth direction can be written as:

<!-- b002180 | prose | pages [8] -->

where x ∈[0, W], W denotes the effective width of the step, and h(x, t) represents the accumulated wear over time at position x.

<!-- b002189 | prose | pages [8] -->
Since environmental factors are inherently uncertain and random, we treat them as a constant term to simplify the analysis.[?] This approach yields a more tractable mathematical model and ultimately leads to the following Improved Probabilistic Wear Equation (IPWE):

<!-- b002203 | prose | pages [8] -->

<!-- b002961 | prose | pages [9] -->
Submodel ii: Improved PWE Considering Single–Parallel Prob- 3.2 ability

<!-- b002962 | prose | pages [9] -->
Single-File Wear Probability Density 3.2.1

<!-- b002963 | prose | pages [9] -->
To simplify the model, suppose the stair width naturally accommodates two people walking side by side; when only one person is walking, the body center c might have probability peaks at W/4, W/2, or 3W/4. However, by our Assumption 4, whenever a single individual walks up, we set the peak of the body center c at W/2, unaffected by the stair width.

<!-- b002964 | prose | pages [9] -->
Under continuous single-file walking, we aim to find a probability density that captures the for- mation of a “W”-shaped depression on the tread: the highest probability occurs at x = W/2, decreasing toward either side. Note that Beta(4, 4) is defined on [0, 1] and can be mapped to [0, W] by multiplying by W. Because Beta(4, 4) is symmetric with a maximum at u = 0.5, it nicely characterizes a center peak at W/2 and tapering tails. Consequently, we choose Beta(4, 4) as the probability density for the body center.[6]

<!-- b003675 | prose | pages [10] -->
Side-by-Side Wear Probability Density 3.2.2

<!-- b003676 | prose | pages [10] -->
When two individuals walk side by side, denote their respective body centers as c1 and c2. Then, for a one-dimensional lateral movement, the probability density of two people walking in parallel is:

<!-- b003677 | prose | pages [10] -->

where w(x; c) is the same function defined for the single-file scenario, C is a normalization constant, and c1, c2 can be fixed or slightly randomized.

<!-- b003689 | prose | pages [10] -->
3.2.3

<!-- b003690 | heading | pages [10] -->
Single-Parallel Wear Model

<!-- b003691 | prose | pages [10] -->
If we consider both single-file and side-by-side scenarios simultaneously, we can more compre- hensively capture complex wear dynamics. Let

<!-- b003692 | prose | pages [10] -->
Φ(x; N) = α(N) ϕdouble(x) + (1 −α(N)) ϕsingle(x),

<!-- b003693 | prose | pages [10] -->
and substitute into the PWE, yielding the single-parallel wear model:

<!-- b003694 | prose | pages [10] -->
∂h

<!-- b003695 | prose | pages [10] -->

<!-- b003696 | prose | pages [10] -->

<!-- b003697 | prose | pages [10] -->
i

<!-- b003698 | prose | pages [10] -->
 1 −α(N)

<!-- b003699 | prose | pages [10] -->
∂t (x, t) = κ N

<!-- b003700 | prose | pages [10] -->
α(N) ϕdouble(x) +

<!-- b003701 | prose | pages [10] -->
ϕsingle(x)

<!-- b003702 | prose | pages [10] -->
+ β, h(x, 0) = 0, (15)

<!-- b003703 | prose | pages [10] -->
where α(N) denotes the frequency of “side-by-side” occurrences under arrival intensity N, and 1−α(N) denotes the frequency of “single-file” occurrences.

<!-- b003704 | prose | pages [10] -->
3.2.4 Issue of Usage Density

<!-- b003705 | prose | pages [10] -->
Next, we investigate the frequency of staircase usage, specifically whether there was a period of intensive use over a short term or sparse usage over a long term. Our basic assumption is that a sudden, high-volume crowd would force most people to walk side by side on the stairs due to the crowded environment. Hence, a key consideration is how the arrival intensity N affects the parallel-walking probability α(N).

<!-- b003706 | prose | pages [10] -->
First, α is expected to increase with the arrival intensity N. Meanwhile, α should neither vanish at N →0 nor approach unity at N →∞. Realistically, even with very low foot traffic, there may still be individuals walking side by side, and at very high traffic, a certain portion of people still opt to walk single file. Moreover, we need α(N) to be continuously differentiable for stable gradient calculations in PDE solutions and parameter estimation, and it should be parameterizable with a small number of variables to fit real data.

<!-- b004400 | caption | pages [12] -->
Figure 6: Wear patterns under different crowd densities.

<!-- b004401 | prose | pages [12] -->
Foot Partitioning in Upstairs and Downstairs In this subsection, we examine the biome- chanical and practical reasons for partitioning the foot differently when navigating upstairs versus downstairs.

<!-- b004402 | caption | pages [12] -->
Figure 7: Motion diagram when up-/downstairs.

<!-- b004403 | heading | pages [12] -->
1. Biomechanical Differences When Walking Upstairs vs. Downstairs:

<!-- b004404 | prose | pages [12] -->
To overcome gravity and raise the body upwards, the center of mass typically shifts forward when going upstairs, causing the front portion of the foot (front 2/3) to bear more vertical re- action force during take-off and stair contact. Conversely, walking downstairs requires energy absorption and controlled descent; the center of mass leans slightly backward, and the heel and rear portion of the foot (rear 2/3) shoulder the brunt of the impact, exhibiting higher landing frequency and force concentration on the rear foot.

<!-- b004405 | heading | pages [12] -->
2. Differential Wear and Front/Rear 2/3 Partitioning:

<!-- b004406 | prose | pages [12] -->
Using a single “entire-foot” contact profile cannot capture the marked discrepancy between “front-foot dominance upstairs, rear-foot dominance downstairs,” leading to inaccuracies in sim- ulating wear on the front vs. rear edges of a stair. To better represent these biomechanical differ- ences, we employ binary images of the front 2/3 mapped to ϕup(x, y) (upstairs) and the rear 2/3 mapped to ϕdown(x, y) (downstairs), each corresponding to its primary load-bearing zone.

<!-- b004407 | heading | pages [12] -->
3. Gaussian Blurring and Random Shifts:

<!-- b004408 | prose | pages [12] -->
To more closely approximate continuous foot-pressure distribution and the variability of foot

<!-- b004409 | prose | pages [12] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b004410 | furniture | pages [12] -->
2

<!-- b004411 | furniture | pages [12] -->
MATHmodels

<!-- b004412 | furniture | pages [12] -->
获取更多资讯

<!-- b004413 | furniture | pages [13] -->
Team # 2504218

<!-- b004414 | furniture | pages [13] -->
Page 12 of 25

<!-- b004415 | prose | pages [13] -->
placement, both binary images undergo Gaussian blurring to achieve smooth transitions be- tween the foot and the stair. Multiple small random shifts are then applied in the stair coor- dinate system to replicate the fact that each step is not identically placed, ultimately yielding a smooth and more realistic wear pattern.

<!-- b004416 | prose | pages [13] -->
3.3.1 Construction of Foot Distributions and Minor Shifts

<!-- b004417 | prose | pages [13] -->
A detailed description of how these front/rear foot partitions are incorporated into the stair coor- dinate system and how random shifts are applied is given below.

<!-- b004418 | prose | pages [13] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b004419 | prose | pages [13] -->
In practice, we first obtain two binary masks—one from the “front two-thirds” and one from the “rear two-thirds” of a foot image—where white represents the background and black the foot region. Denoting these masks by footup(x′, y′) footdown(x′, y′), with (x′, y′) being image coordinates, and we then apply Gaussian blurring, scaling, and positional fitting to map them into the stair coordinate system (x, y). Multiple small random shifts and noise can be superimposed, yielding smoothed contact distributions ϕup(x, y) and ϕdown(x, y).

<!-- b004420 | prose | pages [13] -->
To obtain a smoother foot distribution, the following steps are applied to each binary foot mask:

<!-- b004421 | heading | pages [13] -->
1. Gaussian Blurring: Convolve the mask with a Gaussian kernel of width σblur to smooth foot

<!-- b004422 | prose | pages [13] -->
edges.

<!-- b004423 | heading | pages [13] -->
2. Scaling and Fitting: Map image coordinates onto a small region of the stair domain Ω(for in-

<!-- b004424 | prose | pages [13] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b004425 | prose | pages [13] -->
stance, aligned with the bottom edge or centered), introducing a random perturbation if needed.

<!-- b004426 | heading | pages [13] -->
3. Repeated Random Offsets: Overlay the foot mask multiple times, each with a small offset

<!-- b004427 | prose | pages [13] -->
(δx, δy), and then normalize the sum to obtain the final ϕup or ϕdown.

<!-- b004428 | prose | pages [13] -->
Here, (δx, δy) can be drawn from a Gaussian distribution N((x0, y0), Σ) to reflect that each footstep may not fall exactly in the same spot. The cumulative distribution—after normalization—provides a foot-contact probability map over the stair surface. Specific algorithm is demonstrated below.

<!-- b004429 | heading | pages [13] -->
Algorithm 1: Build Foot Distribution (Gaussian + Random Offsets)

<!-- b004432 | heading | pages [13] -->

<!-- b004433 | heading | pages [13] -->

<!-- b004467 | heading | pages [14] -->
Foot Partition-based Travel Direction Model (FPTDM)

<!-- b004468 | prose | pages [14] -->
The proposed FPTDM relies on the following observation: when walking upstairs, the primary load-bearing and contact area of the foot is in the front two-thirds, whereas when walking downstairs, the main load-bearing region shifts to the rear two-thirds. By extracting the contact patterns from these two binary images—one representing the “front two-thirds” of the foot and the other the “rear two-thirds”—and incorporating flow rates of people going upstairs and downstairs, we can formulate a partial differential equation that describes staircase wear.

<!-- b004469 | caption | pages [14] -->
Figure 8: Foot partition.

<!-- b004470 | prose | pages [14] -->
Consider a stair tread represented by a planar region Ω⊂R2 with coordinates (x, y). Let h(x, y, t) be the wear depth (or height loss) at time t. Suppose Nup(t) and Ndown(t) are the numbers of people going upstairs and downstairs, respectively (they may vary over time or be approximated as constants). A linear wear equation is then given by

<!-- b004471 | prose | pages [14] -->


<!-- b004472 | prose | pages [14] -->
∂h

<!-- b004473 | prose | pages [14] -->
h

<!-- b004474 | prose | pages [14] -->
i

<!-- b004475 | prose | pages [14] -->
Nup(t) ϕup(x, y) + Ndown(t) ϕdown(x, y)

<!-- b004476 | prose | pages [14] -->
∂t (x, y, t) = κ

<!-- b004477 | prose | pages [14] -->


<!-- b004478 | prose | pages [14] -->
h(x, y, 0) = 0,

<!-- b004479 | prose | pages [14] -->


<!-- b004480 | prose | pages [14] -->
+ β,

<!-- b004481 | prose | pages [14] -->
(18)

<!-- b004482 | prose | pages [14] -->
where κ is the material wear coefficient, β is an environmental constant accounting for uniform wear from factors such as weathering or water erosion, and ϕup(x, y), ϕdown(x, y) are the contact distributions for the front two-thirds (upstairs) and rear two-thirds (downstairs) portions of the foot, respectively.

<!-- b004483 | prose | pages [14] -->
To solve (18),we can employ an explicit Euler scheme (assuming no diffusion or adjacent cou- pling):

<!-- b004484 | prose | pages [14] -->


<!-- b004485 | prose | pages [14] -->
(

<!-- b004486 | prose | pages [14] -->
hn+1[j, i] = hn[j, i] + ∆t

<!-- b004487 | prose | pages [14] -->


<!-- b004488 | prose | pages [14] -->
κ Nup(tn) ϕup(xi, yj) + κ Ndown(tn) ϕdown(xi, yj) + β

<!-- b004489 | prose | pages [14] -->
h0[j, i] = 0.

<!-- b004490 | prose | pages [14] -->
,

<!-- b004491 | prose | pages [14] -->
(19)

<!-- b004492 | prose | pages [14] -->
By iterating until n = T, one obtains the staircase wear depth hT [j, i] at final time T. For a clearer explanation of our solution approach, we present the adopted algorithm steps below.

<!-- b004493 | heading | pages [14] -->
Algorithm 2: Explicit Euler Scheme for FPTDM

<!-- b004494 | prose | pages [14] -->
Data: Inputs:

<!-- b004495 | prose | pages [14] -->
accum[j, i]: foot contact distribution, (T, N, β): PDE parameters (time steps, usage, environment), κ: material wear coefficient, ∆t: time step.

<!-- b004496 | prose | pages [14] -->
Final wear depth map h(j, i) on the (Nx × Ny) grid.

<!-- b004497 | heading | pages [14] -->
Result:

<!-- b004498 | heading | pages [14] -->
1 h ←0(Ny,Nx);

<!-- b004499 | heading | pages [14] -->
2 for t ←1 to T do

<!-- b004500 | prose | pages [14] -->
foreach cell (i, j) in the grid do 3

<!-- b004501 | prose | pages [14] -->
usage[j, i] ←κ × N × accum[j, i]; 4

<!-- b004502 | prose | pages [14] -->
 

<!-- b004503 | prose | pages [14] -->
h[j, i] ←h[j, i] + ∆t ×

<!-- b004504 | prose | pages [14] -->
usage[j, i] + β

<!-- b004505 | prose | pages [14] -->
5

<!-- b004506 | prose | pages [14] -->
end 6

<!-- b004507 | heading | pages [14] -->
7 end

<!-- b004508 | heading | pages [14] -->
8 Output: final h(j, i) over the ladder.

<!-- b004519 | caption | pages [15] -->
Figure 9: Ascending wear evolution graph.

<!-- b004520 | caption | pages [15] -->
Figure 10: Descending wear evolution graph.

<!-- b004521 | prose | pages [15] -->
Next, we present the solution results, providing a visual demonstration of the differences between ascending and descending wear patterns to verify the reasonableness and effectiveness of our findings. Comparing Figure 9 and Figure 10, we can see that the indentation center for the ascending wear lies farther from the stair edge, covers a larger area, and is shallower, whereas the descending wear shows the opposite trend.

<!-- b004522 | prose | pages [15] -->
To more clearly illustrate the differences in wear patterns between ascending and descending us- age, we provide four comparative figures from various angles below.

<!-- b004523 | prose | pages [15] -->
If Nup(t) and Ndown(t) vary over time, we simply update their values at each time step tn. If they are roughly constant, we may directly use fixed Nup and Ndown.

<!-- b004524 | prose | pages [15] -->
In short, by combining the front two-thirds foot image (upstairs) and the rear two-thirds foot image (downstairs) to construct ϕup and ϕdown, then applying Gaussian blurring and multiple random offsets within the stair coordinate system, we obtain a smooth contact distribution. Solving the linear wear equation (via an explicit Euler method) yields the time-evolving wear depth h(x, y, t). Minor random offsets and noise make footstep patterns more realistic and, over a long simulation, aggregate

<!-- b005197 | heading | pages [16] -->
Model II: Wear Parameter Inversion

<!-- b005198 | prose | pages [16] -->
4

<!-- b005199 | prose | pages [16] -->
In the context of a non-diffusion linear wear equation, we aim to invert multiple parameters and quantify uncertainties. Given that the measured data are typically noisy and that the stair wear profile often requires smooth transitions, this work integrates prior distributions and second-order gradient smoothing regularization into the Bayesian inference. Subsequently, it employs an improved strategy with a heuristic-based preliminary search to accelerate convergence to the global optimum.

<!-- b005200 | prose | pages [16] -->
Bayesian inference and smoothing regularization[7] 4.1

<!-- b005201 | prose | pages [16] -->
Let (x, y) ∈Ω⊂R2 be the stair plane coordinates, and t ∈[0, T]. Denote the parameters to be estimated by Θ = (κ, N, T, . . . ). The non-diffusion linear wear equation is given in (??). To incorporate archaeological and materials-related expert knowledge, we specify a Bayesian prior in the parameter space:

<!-- b005202 | prose | pages [16] -->
p(Θ) = pκ(κ) pN(N) pT (T) · · · ,

<!-- b005203 | prose | pages [16] -->
where pκ(κ) etc. are the individual prior distributions for each parameter, determined from literature and experience.

<!-- b005204 | prose | pages [16] -->
Suppose that at time t = T, a numerical or analytic method provides hsim(x, y; Θ), while real observations are available only at a set of points (xi, yi), yielding noisy measurements ˆhobs(xi, yi) (i = 1, 2 . . . , m). At each observation point (xi, yi), define the observed height of wear as

<!-- b005205 | prose | pages [16] -->
ˆhobs(xi, yi) = hsim(xi, yi; Θ) + εi, εi ∼N(0, σ2),

<!-- b005206 | prose | pages [16] -->
where parameter sigma is given according to experience or knowledge.

<!-- b005207 | prose | pages [16] -->
If the solution hsim is required to exhibit a smooth indentation shape, a smoothing penalty can be added to the objective function:

<!-- b005209 | prose | pages [16] -->

Subsequently, we perform Markov Chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling under the posterior (??), seeking high-posterior parameter sets that are consistent with expert knowledge and actual measure-

<!-- b005251 | prose | pages [17] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b005252 | prose | pages [17] -->
ments.

<!-- b005253 | prose | pages [17] -->
Finally, we use a Quantile-based Method to construct credible intervals. Specifically, we sort the parameter samples drawn from the posterior distribution and select the sample values at the relevant quantiles to determine the interval endpoints.

<!-- b005254 | caption | pages [17] -->
Figure 12: xxx.

<!-- b005255 | heading | pages [17] -->
Improved strategy: Preliminary search via heuristic algorithm

<!-- b005256 | prose | pages [17] -->
4.2

<!-- b005257 | prose | pages [17] -->
MCMC searches for an optimal solution that aligns with both the prior and the smoothness con- straint. From equations (??)–(22), it follows that the MCMC optimum is in fact the solution to the optimization problem

<!-- b005258 | prose | pages [17] -->


<!-- b005259 | furniture | pages [17] -->


<!-- b005260 | prose | pages [17] -->
−ln p(Θ) + E(Θ)

<!-- b005261 | prose | pages [17] -->
min

<!-- b005262 | prose | pages [17] -->
Θ

<!-- b005263 | prose | pages [17] -->

<!-- b005264 | prose | pages [17] -->
(23)

<!-- b005265 | prose | pages [17] -->
However, directly applying global MCMC sampling to p(Θ | ˆh) in a high-dimensional parameter space is typically expensive and slow to converge. To rapidly find a global optimum or near-global optimum Θ∗to problem (23), one can employ a heuristic algorithm (e.g., PSO or GA).[8] Therefore, we propose a combined approach for improvement:

<!-- b005266 | heading | pages [17] -->
1. Use a heuristic algorithm (taking Particle Swarm Optimization, PSO, as an example) to perform

<!-- b005267 | prose | pages [17] -->
a global search under minΘ E(Θ), thus obtaining a near-global optimal solution Θ∗.

<!-- b006025 | prose | pages [18] -->

<!-- b006045 | prose | pages [19] -->

Repeating this procedure for other parameters {a, b, N, t, . . . } provides intervals and quantifies their uncertainty in a straightforward manner.

<!-- b006063 | prose | pages [19] -->
By means of this two-step process, we achieve an integrated scheme of “global optimization + posterior uncertainty quantification” under limited computational overhead. It aligns the simulated results with observations, maintains surface smoothness, and leverages prior knowledge to ensure con- sistency with physical and materials constraints. Numerical evidence indicates that this method has broad potential applications in archaeological and engineering contexts.

<!-- b006064 | prose | pages [19] -->
4.3

<!-- b006065 | heading | pages [19] -->
Application and Solution

<!-- b006066 | prose | pages [19] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b006067 | prose | pages [19] -->
In this section, we adopt the “Bayesian priors + second-order gradient smoothing” model and the two-step approach (“PSO, then MCMC”) to invert key stair-wear parameters. We use “virtual ob- servations” with added noise from the previous section, simulating uncertainties in foot traffic and measurement accuracy. We then present the parameter setup, the inversion steps, and final results. Lastly, we discuss the error analysis and underlying rationale.

<!-- b006068 | heading | pages [19] -->
1. Simulated Observation Data: True Values and Noise Handling

<!-- b006069 | heading | pages [19] -->
1. Stair Construction Year T ∗: Chosen to be about 257 years, indicating the stair has been in use for

<!-- b006070 | prose | pages [19] -->
257 years. In the inversion, we allow T ∈[240, 280] to reflect the fact that archaeological records only provide a rough range.

<!-- b006071 | heading | pages [19] -->
2. Material Coefficient κ∗: Selected as κ∗= 7.2 × 10−5, with a prior range [10−5, 10−4] (LogUni-

<!-- b006072 | prose | pages [19] -->
form).

5. Forward Simulation + Noise: On a (xi, yi) grid, we solve the 3D PDE until t = T ∗= 257,

<!-- b006119 | prose | pages [20] -->
yielding hsim,true(xi, yi). We then add Gaussian noise ϵi ∼N(0, 0.0122) (standard deviation 0.012), ˆhobs(xi, yi) = hsim,true(xi, yi) + ϵi, sampling m = 8000 points to serve as observation data in the subsequent inversion test.

<!-- b006141 | prose | pages [20] -->
∆2hsim(Θ)[i, j]

<!-- b006142 | prose | pages [20] -->
 

<!-- b006143 | prose | pages [20] -->
+ λ

<!-- b006144 | prose | pages [20] -->
i,j

<!-- b006145 | prose | pages [20] -->
where ∆2h denotes the discrete second-order Laplacian, λ = 5 × 103 (as an example), and p(Θ) is the joint prior probability described above. Thus, it balances data fidelity, tread-surface smoothness, and archaeological prior information.

<!-- b006146 | heading | pages [20] -->
3. Numerical Procedure: PSO + MCMC

<!-- b006147 | heading | pages [20] -->
1. PSO Global Search: We set the particle number P = 40 and run 90 iterations; the process takes

<!-- b006148 | prose | pages [20] -->
about 25 minutes (with a medium-size grid on an Intel i7 CPU). The algorithm converges around iteration 60–70, yielding a global optimum Θ∗.

<!-- b006149 | heading | pages [20] -->
2. MCMC Local Sampling: Centered on Θ∗, we build a normal proposal q(Θ′|Θ) and perform

<!-- b006150 | prose | pages [20] -->
1.0 × 104 Metropolis–Hastings steps, discarding the first 2000 steps as burn-in. This yields a posterior distribution characterizing parameter uncertainty.

<!-- b006151 | heading | pages [20] -->
4. Inversion Results: Table and Comparison

<!-- b006152 | caption | pages [20] -->
Table 2 summarizes the posterior means (using 90% confidence intervals) of the main parameters,

<!-- b006153 | prose | pages [20] -->
alongside their true values.

<!-- b006154 | prose | pages [20] -->
Except for a few parameters (e.g., α(d)

<!-- b006155 | prose | pages [20] -->
min) whose relative error reaches about 16.7%, most lie within a 10% error margin, indicating that the method successfully recovers major parameters even under noise standard deviation σ = 0.012. Key quantities such as T, κ, and parallel usage bounds remain close to their true values.

<!-- b006156 | heading | pages [20] -->
5. Error Distribution and Surface Comparison

<!-- b006157 | prose | pages [20] -->
Substituting the posterior mean back into the forward model yields hsim(x, y). A pointwise com- parison with ˆhobs shows that for most grid points,

<!-- b006158 | prose | pages [20] -->
hsim(x, y)−ˆhobs(x, y)

<!-- b006159 | prose | pages [20] -->
< 0.015, and the overall tread surface is smooth without abrupt spikes. This supports the idea that second-order gradient smoothing effectively suppresses high-frequency noise, and the Bayesian prior enforces consistency with archaeo- logical expectations.

<!-- b006160 | heading | pages [20] -->
6. Discussion and Conclusions

<!-- b006161 | prose | pages [20] -->
• Practical Value: Although this test used “virtual data,” the procedure—prior setting, smoothing, and a two-phase optimization/sampling—can be seamlessly applied to real stair measurements (e.g., from laser scans). It promises similarly robust parameter estimates and reliable uncertainty quantification in actual archaeological settings.

<!-- b006183 | prose | pages [21] -->
• Historical/Preservation Impact: By quantitatively inferring the stair’s construction year T, usage frequencies N(t), and parallel probabilities, we gain deeper insight into the origin of certain tread depressions, aiding future maintenance or conservation strategies.

<!-- b006184 | prose | pages [21] -->
• Role of the Smoothness Term: Without ∇2h regularization, random noise would induce un- physical high-frequency oscillations in the wear-depth. Imposing an appropriate smoothness weight significantly mitigates such artifacts, yielding a more physically plausible wear surface.

<!-- b006185 | prose | pages [21] -->
Overall, these inversion results demonstrate that our “Bayesian + smoothing + PSO + MCMC” ap- proach can stably recover multiple parameters (including T, κ, parallel usage ratio, and up/down foot- traffic) with acceptable accuracy, even with moderate noise in the observational data, illustrating both the effectiveness and potential real-world applicability of this modeling strategy.

<!-- b006186 | heading | pages [21] -->
Model III: Refurbishment and Typical One-day Wear

<!-- b006187 | prose | pages [21] -->
5

<!-- b006188 | prose | pages [21] -->
In actual archaeological contexts, staircases are often subjected to a number of renovations or new treads, resulting in a "multi-layered" structure: traces of damage to the old layer are covered by the new layer, but not completely removed; when the new layer is worn down again, the old layer is re-exposed and is once again subjected to treading.

<!-- b006189 | prose | pages [21] -->
Based on the above situation, we use Multi-layer structural PDE to provide historical analysis of building restoration. Strengths of the Multi-layer structural PDE are as follows:

<!-- b006190 | prose | pages [21] -->
• Realistic repair process: The model reflects the typical repair practice of adding new layers over old ones, rather than resetting wear marks, providing a more accurate representation of renovation processes.

<!-- b006191 | prose | pages [21] -->
• Preservation of underlying layers: If the new layer remains intact, the lower layer is unaffected.

<!-- b006192 | prose | pages [21] -->
When the top layer is worn or removed, the lower layer is exposed and resumes accumulating wear, mirroring real-life stair repair dynamics.

<!-- b006193 | prose | pages [21] -->
• Archaeological consistency: The multi-layer structure aligns with actual archaeological profiles, where each layer has distinct materials or thicknesses, ensuring the model’s relevance to real- world data.

<!-- b006883 | prose | pages [22] -->
• Handling free boundaries and complexity: The model’s use of multi-layer PDEs with hierarchi- cal activation mechanisms accurately captures phase transitions, enabling advanced studies of stability, uniqueness, and inverse problems.

<!-- b006884 | prose | pages [22] -->
5.1 Multi-layer structural PDE

<!-- b006885 | prose | pages [22] -->
Let the steps have a total of m+1 layers throughout their history of use and renovation: L0, L1, . . . , Lm; where L0 is the original layer and Lm is the current top layer. Notation:

<!-- b006886 | prose | pages [22] -->
is the cumulative wear depth of the ith layer at moment t. hi(x, y, t) (24)

<!-- b006887 | prose | pages [22] -->
Each layer has an initial thickness δi > 0. When hi < δi, the layer is not yet worn through; if hi reaches δi, the layer is declared to be worn through at the point (x, y), and the layer below begins to be exposed.

<!-- b006888 | prose | pages [22] -->
5.1.1 Worn-out equation

<!-- b006889 | prose | pages [22] -->
In the absence of renovation disturbances, the ith layer of material is subject to the combined effects of foot traffic and the environment with the following wear equation:

<!-- b006890 | prose | pages [22] -->
∂hi

<!-- b006891 | prose | pages [22] -->


<!-- b006892 | prose | pages [22] -->


<!-- b006893 | prose | pages [22] -->
N(t) ϕi(x, y)

<!-- b006894 | prose | pages [22] -->
∂t (x, y, t) = κi

<!-- b006895 | prose | pages [22] -->
+ Fenv(x, y, t) (25)

<!-- b006896 | prose | pages [22] -->
where κi is the wear coefficient of the material in the layer, N(t) is the volume of foot traffic, ϕi(x, y) denotes the tread distribution (which may be related to the same layer i or may be unchanged), and Fenv is an additional factor such as environmental weathering.

<!-- b006897 | prose | pages [22] -->
However, this PDE only takes effect when "layer i is uppermost and exposed"; if there is another layer i + 1 above it that is being covered, layer i no longer accumulates wear.

<!-- b006898 | prose | pages [22] -->
5.1.2 Renovate (add new layer)

<!-- b006899 | prose | pages [22] -->
After that, if there is no new layer covering, layer Li+1 becomes the "active layer" and remains so until the next layer Li+2 appears or the layer is worn through.

<!-- b006900 | prose | pages [22] -->
At certain specific moments tr1, tr2, . . . , trm, layers are added step by step from the 1st layer to the mth layer. Specifically: when t = tr(i+1), layer Li+1 is added, and the wear depth of that layer is set to hi+1(x, y, tr(i+1)) = 0, with δi+1 being the initial thickness of that layer. After that, if there is no new layer covering, layer Li+1 becomes the "active layer" and remains so until the next layer Li+2 appears or the layer is worn through.

<!-- b006901 | prose | pages [22] -->
5.1.3 Penetration conditions

<!-- b006902 | prose | pages [22] -->
Each layer behaves as follows:

<!-- b006903 | prose | pages [22] -->
(

<!-- b006904 | prose | pages [22] -->
hi(x, y, t) < δi ⇒The layer remains intact and can still be stepped on, unless covered by a layer above it. hi(x, y, t) ≥δi ⇒This layer at (x, y) has been worn through, exposing the layer underneath.

<!-- b006905 | prose | pages [22] -->
If a layer Lj overlaps above Li, then Li will not accumulate any further wear during this time. Once Lj is completely worn out or removed, Li will once again become the "top layer" and continue to accumulate wear.

<!-- b006906 | prose | pages [22] -->
In numerical simulations, time can be discretized conventionally as t0 = 0 < t1 < · · · < tN = T, and the following steps are performed at each time step:

<!-- b006907 | heading | pages [22] -->

<!-- b006908 | prose | pages [22] -->

staircase. We use a pictorial approach to deal with this problem:

<!-- b006923 | prose | pages [24] -->
• Image Loading:Prepare a binary or grayscale "foot image" with a foot area of 1 and a background of 0.

<!-- b006924 | prose | pages [24] -->
• Gaussian Blur Processing: To avoid overly sharp edges on the sole, we apply Gaussian blur to the sole image:

<!-- b006925 | prose | pages [24] -->
foot_blur(u, v) = 1

<!-- b006937 | prose | pages [24] -->
• Random Offset: When each pedestrian event e occurs, we perform random rotation and/or translation on the footprint map to simulate slightly different stepping positions of pedestrians on the stairs. <!-- b006944 | prose | pages [24] -->
y and θe denote the random perturbations of the translational and rotational angles

<!-- b006945 | prose | pages [24] -->
where ∆e

<!-- b006946 | prose | pages [24] -->

<!-- b006947 | prose | pages [24] -->
in the x, y directions, respectively.

<!-- b006948 | prose | pages [24] -->
In the discrete event simulation, each pedestrian event e has a Total Wear Factor ηe > 0, which represents the average depth of wear for a "single footprint" (or for some materials, ηe is a small con- stant). The footprint map foot_diste(x, y) is then multiplied by ηe to obtain the contribution of the event e to the wear at each point of the staircase:

<!-- b006949 | prose | pages [24] -->
∆he(x, y) = ηe · foot_diste(x, y).

<!-- b006950 | prose | pages [24] -->
(31)

<!-- b006951 | prose | pages [24] -->
At the end of the day, we can sum all events e ∈E to obtain the total wear for the day:

<!-- b006952 | prose | pages [24] -->
X

<!-- b006953 | prose | pages [24] -->
X

<!-- b006954 | prose | pages [24] -->
ηe · foot_diste(x, y).

<!-- b006955 | prose | pages [24] -->
∆he(x, y) =

<!-- b006956 | prose | pages [24] -->
hsim(x, y) =

<!-- b006957 | prose | pages [24] -->
e∈E

<!-- b006958 | prose | pages [24] -->
(32)

<!-- b006959 | prose | pages [24] -->
e∈E

<!-- b006960 | prose | pages [24] -->
In this way, by means of image superimposition, we are able to accurately model the wear contribution of each pedestrian to the staircase, and ultimately obtain the total wear of the entire staircase.

<!-- b006961 | prose | pages [24] -->
The DES-based model provides a robust framework for capturing the dynamic and spatial pat- terns of wear induced by foot traffic. By incorporating an arrival rate function and Poisson event gen- eration, along with Gaussian blur and random offsets, the model accurately simulates how both con- centrated and dispersed foot traffic contribute to stair wear. This approach enables a high-resolution, real-time analysis of daily traffic patterns, offering a significant improvement over traditional macro- level models. It provides a more precise representation of wear distribution, making it a valuable tool for both academic research and practical applications, particularly in fields such as transportation, in- frastructure management, and urban planning.

<!-- b006962 | prose | pages [24] -->
6

<!-- b006963 | heading | pages [24] -->
Sensitivity Analysis

<!-- b006964 | prose | pages [24] -->
In this sensitivity analysis, the model fixes two crowd modes—“short-term large usage” and “long-term small usage”—while incrementally varying the material wear coefficient κ from 1 × 10−7 to 3 × 10−7. The results indicate that, for both modes, the greatest indentation emerges around the center of the step, and the final wear depth increases overall as κ grows. In the “long-term small usage” mode, wear accumulates more evenly, and the differences among curves at various κ values tend to scale in a near-multiplicative manner. By contrast, “short-term large usage” involves a burst of concentrated foot traffic that ceases once the short period ends, producing more discrete intervals of wear and pre- venting a simple linear or multiplicative growth pattern in the final curve. In conclusion, the model

<!-- b006965 | prose | pages [24] -->
关注数学模型

<!-- b006966 | furniture | pages [24] -->
2

<!-- b006967 | furniture | pages [24] -->
MATHmodels

<!-- b006968 | furniture | pages [24] -->

<!-- b006969 | furniture | pages [25] -->

<!-- b006970 | furniture | pages [25] -->
Page 24 of 25

<!-- b006971 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信公众号：数学模型

<!-- b006972 | caption | pages [25] -->
Figure 14: Sensitivity analysis for κ and αshort.

<!-- b006973 | prose | pages [25] -->
remains highly sensitive to κ, indicating that precise calibration of this parameter is crucial for avoiding significant prediction errors arising from minor estimation deviations.

<!-- b006974 | prose | pages [25] -->
In this model, αshort represents the probability of side-by-side walking under a “short-term large usage” scenario. In the long-term small usage setting, where the flow is spread over a longer period, changes in αshort have a minimal impact on the final wear, resulting in nearly indistinguishable curves and

<!-- b006975 | prose | pages [25] -->
微信号：MATHmodels

<!-- b006976 | prose | pages [25] -->
indicating the parameter’s insensitivity in that context. Conversely, if one focuses solely on the short- term large usage scenario, αshort becomes highly influential in determining the concentration of foot traffic: a higher side-by-side probability leads to more pronounced and rapid indentation of the tread center within a short time frame. Consequently, variations in αshort under short-term large usage can significantly affect the resulting indentation shape and depth.

<!-- b006977 | prose | pages [25] -->
Moreover, we also conducted a sensitivity analysis on other model parameters, but the complexity of their interactions prevents a detailed discussion here; in brief, we find the model to be highly sensitive to the total simulation time T and the arrival rate N, among others.

<!-- b006978 | prose | pages [25] -->
7

<!-- b006979 | heading | pages [25] -->
Strengths and Weaknesses

<!-- b006980 | prose | pages [25] -->
7.1

<!-- b006981 | heading | pages [25] -->
Strengths

<!-- b006982 | prose | pages [25] -->
• The paper integrates different sub-models (including single/parallel usage, multi-layer PDE, and discrete-event simulation) to handle both daily and long-term stair wear scenarios.

<!-- b006983 | prose | pages [25] -->
• By assigning prior distributions to key parameters, the approach incorporates archaeological knowledge and refines estimates of wear rates, usage ratios, and material properties.

<!-- b006984 | prose | pages [25] -->
• Particle Swarm Optimization provides a global optimum, while MCMC around that optimum captures parameter uncertainty and produces a posterior distribution.

<!-- b006985 | prose | pages [25] -->
• By applying Gaussian blurring and random offset, the final 3D wear-depth distribution better mimics actual footprints and daily repairs.

<!-- b006986 | prose | pages [25] -->
7.2

<!-- b006987 | heading | pages [25] -->
Weaknesses

<!-- b006988 | prose | pages [25] -->
• The combined use of multiple sub-models and Bayesian priors requires managing many param- eters, which can be computationally demanding.

<!-- b006989 | prose | pages [25] -->
• Accurate 3D stair-surface data are essential. Sparse or low-quality measurements limit the preci- sion of the PDE and probabilistic models.

<!-- b006990 | prose | pages [25] -->
• Imposing a second-order gradient penalty enforces global smoothness but might overlook sud- den wear anomalies or abrupt material changes.

<!-- b006991 | heading | pages [25] -->
